    The Moss Man

    Chapter Summary

    Lena receives a surprise.

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Sixteen: The Moss Man

     

    “Here, try it with a little more angle…” Kara said as she scooped her arms behind Lena’s and then held the back of her hand with a ninety-degree turn. “Like this.”

    The flat stone in Lena’s hand was maybe half the size of her palm, but it was trapezoidal and good for skipping like many of the others that were scattered around their feet. The muddy banks of the Caddo weren’t exactly what you’d call “rocky” like some of the rivers were further west— but there were always good skipping stones if one knew where to look. Arrowheads were abundant on these sandy shores as well, especially beneath the occasional uprooted tree. Kara liked to leave them in piles near the entryways to her home; blessings of protection for those either coming or going.

    Kara gave a slight bounce of Lena’s wrist with a laugh, “You’re too stiff, loosen up a bit.”

    “Hey, I am doing my best here…”

    “That’s the problem, you’re trying too hard. Don’t try. Just do.”

    Lena laughed. “Well, if it was that easy, then—”

    Kara flung Lena’s wrist with the stone out towards the top of the water. It bounced three times in quick succession and then skittered to a stop along its calm surface before plunking downward.

    “See?”

    Lena was squinting towards the lily pad harvested part of their shoreline with a hard pinch to her face, as if she were deep in thought. The genius-level gears were turning with abundance behind her eyes. Kara dropped her hands to her sides…

    “Are you doing math right now?”

    “Yes.”

    “I dunno…” Kara scratched at her head, “Seems sacrilegious—”

    “Let me try again?”

    “Okay…” Obliging the woman she loved another go, Kara bent down and washed a stone in the shallow water before handing it over.

    Lena squared her feet and then ran her eyes back and forth between her hand and the top of the water, and with a precise -flick- of her wrist and simultaneous -twist- of her hips, the stone went out to kiss the surface of the lake in a series of long, repetitive skips. It went on so far that Kara had nearly lost sight of it. Her jaw felt like hitting the ground.

    Lena turned to look back at Kara with an accomplished sort of grin upon her face. “How was that?”

    “Wait—” Kara bent quickly to root around for another stone, “Wait a minute.”

    She tried skipping a few of her own after that, but none of them quite shaped up to Lena’s last attempt. Kara was impressed and flummoxed all at the same time.

    Lena chuckled beneath her breath and gave an endearing pat to the low hanging part of Kara’s jaw. “Come on. You said we could go get lunch soon.”

    “Just a sec.” Kara insisted upon washing and throwing a few more, all terminating at the same lengths across the water. Her absolute bests. “How did—”

    Lena’s laughing pulled Kara’s focus again, and there a smile was found to spread across her face. Kara would always enjoy the sound of Lena’s laughter, especially if she were the cause of it. It was never at her expense. She bubbled into a laugh of her own, and then they were holding each other with a happy and heavy embrace.

    It would soon come to a crashing halt.

    Out of nowhere, something like a blood-curdling scream straight from the depths of hell came out from the woods nearby. A murder of crows burst into a glossy, black-feathered flight from the treetops with it, disturbed from their mid-day brooding. They cawed noisily overhead as the screaming came forth from the wood again.

    Lena’s face had gone absolutely white, bereft of any color. “What in the—”

    “Stop.” Kara warned once Lena went to dart towards the trees.

    The screaming came a third time, longer and more profuse than those that had come before it, and Lena finally twisted around to insist upon her sense of urgency, “Kara, someone’s in there—”  

    “Just, wait.”

    “They need help—!”

    “It’s not what you think.”

    Lena’s feet came to stand still with that. “What do you mean?”

    Kara craned her head to look further towards the tree line and paused to consider the risk. She could just show her. From a safe distance, that is…

    “Alright. But just close enough for you to see.”

    Lena took off towards the woods with a brisk pace, and Kara soon caught up to her. There would always be a steep sense of admiration for Lena’s willingness to help others, but sometimes, Kara felt that she didn’t quite understand the cost of said help. Maybe it was that she simply didn’t care. Lena would give the shirt off her back to anyone whom she felt needed it more. She was selfless like that. But out here, that sort of selflessness could give rise to dangerous situations. Nature would never not have its unforgiving side.

    “Slow down—”

    “Which way was it?” Lena flipped her ponytail back and forth through her search of the trees.

    Kara hooked her hand around the first few young pines as they ventured inside. “We need to be careful right now—”

    Right about then, the screaming sound lashed out once again, and with what was very likely a complete physical response, Lena wheeled to an abrupt stop and started to backpedal almost immediately. Kara would have told her what it was then and there if the smaller, more helpless sounds that followed hadn’t clapped her mouth shut altogether. Her eyes began to search along the ground, looking for the small shape that cried out for mercy. Kara took Lena by the elbow and pulled her close just as a clumsy flash of reddish-brown fur loped in their direction. It cried frantically in its escape, running straight into Lena’s legs with a nosed whimper. Lena gasped as the ball of fur ran to hide behind her and cried some more, shielding itself from whatever it was that gave chase.

    Kara heard the menacing, close-lipped growl as it approached. Just as she suspected. She turned to Lena and waved hurriedly towards the ground, “Pick them up—”

    “What- are you sure?”

    “Pick them up! Hurry!”

    Lena scooped the small pup into her hands, and soon the crying stopped.

    “Get ready to run.”

    “Kara—”

    “…just as soon as I can find it.”

    “Kara, what’s—”

    “There.” Kara said as she pointed towards a large fern. Out from it stepped the stripy face of a bobcat, the long fur of its distinct ruffs and tufted ears bearing down upon them with a vengeance.

    “Oh, God.” Lena whispered as soon as she realized their mistake. She cradled the small, wiggling mass of fur in her arms in an effort to both calm the creature and to ready herself.

    “I should have just told you,” Kara explained, “Bobcat territory screams can sound like humans in distress.”

    “That’s not absolutely terrifying.”

    “Well, now you know.” Kara continued as she backed them up.

    As always, Lena seemed to be the voice of reason. “But we wouldn’t have found him if we hadn’t come looking…”

    Like it or not, fight or flight was the situation now, and with a predator cat of this size, one would almost always want to err on the side of flight. Bobcats weren’t known to pursue a threat beyond the borders of their territory, anyway. They could run, but she had no guarantee that this bobcat wouldn’t follow. It seemed good and pissed off. Kara took an educated guess and supposed that the feline did not take kindly to some wandering pup nosing around too far from its den.

    The bobcat slunk ever slowly towards them still, and Kara decided to put herself directly in its path.

    “No.” She said towards it with a firm voice.

    The pup in Lena’s arms began to whimper again, most likely scared of any new sound other than the familiar whimpers of its litter mates. At least it could hear now, but whether or not it could see remained to be, well, seen.

    As for the bobcat, it may have come to a halt but still it pursued its relentless stare-down with Kara. Lena was shushing the small and frightened creature in her arms, her caretaker nature shining through like always, and Kara was leaned intently forward, one foot planted firmly in front of the other. “Go away.” She commanded.

    And with one last snarl, away the bobcat went.

    The relief between herself and Lena was palpable, to say the least, though, the pup squirming to get free of Lena’s hands hadn’t quite gotten the message just yet. Lena tried a few more gentle coos and hushes until she decided just to start speaking to him.

    “It’s okay! It’s okay- you’re alright. Look…” She bobbed her head in an attempt to catch his attention. The pup still couldn’t get a good fix on her yet, but once he did, he stilled inside of her hands and let all four of his legs hang freely as if struck by the very sight of her. Then, his tail began to wag with abandon. Suddenly, the pup started to cry again, this time in a needy effort to be nearer to her. Lena clung him to her chest once again and smiled with a stark sense of surprise.

    “…Uh oh.” Kara said.

    Lena looked at her with wide eyes, “I have no idea what just happened.”

    “Congratulations, you’re a mom.”

    “…What?” Lena deadpanned.

    “Can you put him down for a sec? I need to see something.”

    Lena bent forward to place the pup back upon the ground, and there he stayed in front of her, tail ever wagging.

    “Now go over there, please…?” Kara said with another point.

    Any amount of distance between them seemed to be too much for the small pup. He cried louder for Lena and followed her with quick and clumsy steps; Once, twice, and then a third and fourth time as she walked away from him in a diamond pattern.

    “Oh.” Lena huffed a breath, “Wow.”

    “Take a good look at his back legs when he walks,” Kara gestured towards the little hopping movements the pup’s hind legs made in unison, “…he’s wolf.”

    Lena paused for a moment with that, and then she gasped, “Do you think—”

    “Dog?”

    “Who else?”

    “I mean,” Kara eyed the pup closer, “He’s gotta be, I guess? The odds are- hell- I dunno.” She kneeled down to pet his soft, almost peach colored fur. “I heard howling on the night I buried Dog. Lots of it.”

    “Is that strange?”

    “Sort of… Coyotes don’t exactly howl. They ‘yip’, more than anything.”

    Lena looked further into the trees once she picked up the pup again. He’d been clawing upward at her legs in order to climb, desperate to be face to face with her again. “So, he’s…”

    “Yours, apparently.” Kara laughed, “Even hybrid wolf pups are born blind and deaf. They get around after the first few weeks just by smell and touch alone. Sometimes they wander off and get lost- but normally, they imprint onto the first living thing they see whenever their vision comes in. Usually it’s a packmate.” Lena smiled with that and hugged the pup to her chest once more, and Kara went on, “You’re the first thing he’s ever seen. So, naturally, you’re the first thing he’s ever loved.”

    Lena’s smile quickly turned down and into a frown. “I don’t suppose that’s the best thing for him…is it? What do I do now?”

    “Well. We need to try and find the rest of his litter…”

    “Okay.”

    “Ready?” Kara asked, taking Lena by the hand to walk deeper into the wood.

    Lena sighed. “As I’ll ever be.”

     

     

    They tried leaving him about twenty yards away from his den once they found it.

    Then maybe fifteen…

    And then ten.

    Kara even tried tucking him inside the earthen shelter that sat beneath a large cypress, but to no avail.

    Every time they tried to take their leave of the wolf pup, he came crying out and running after them as fast as his little legs would take him. From what Kara could count, there were at least four others just like him deep inside of the den, too afraid to do anything other than whine and hide from all the movement up towards the surface. It was lucky that the litter’s mother wasn’t around— Kara wasn’t wholly keen on the idea of learning whether or not she was more coyote or wolf. They were already trespassing upon the natural order of things as it was.

    Eventually, Kara had deigned just to allow the pup to come home with them… But that’s all she could suffer to call him for the time being. “Pup.”

    “Do you think we should get some advice from Alex?” Lena asked from where her face was pressed against the glass of a window at the back of the house. She seemed to be content with watching their guest sleep upon Dog’s mat since he’d taken to it almost immediately, having sniffed his way there as soon as his paws met the wood surface of the back porch.

    Kara tipped her whiskey glass to her lips again and sighed. “I guess.”

    Lena looked over her shoulder towards the couch and frowned, “You’re still upset…”

    “I don’t mean to be.”

    With a graceful turn, Lena came over to sit beside Kara on the couch. “I know, baby. It must be hard.”

    “I just can’t stop thinking about what it’ll feel like.”

    “What do you mean, exactly?”

    Kara clenched her jaw, still adamant in her refusal to cry. She’d not cried for days now, not after having done so for weeks on end. “What if something happens to him?” Her words had barely come out as a whisper.

    “Well, to me, that sounds a little more like ‘what if you get attached to him’ if anything…”

    “I guess.” Kara repeated with another sip from her glass.

    “I’ll leave it up to you, then. What would you like to do?”

    “I just- Oh, I don’t know yet.”

    Kara was conflicted alright. All of it felt like a huge betrayal for many reasons. Taking the pup from the security of his pack, for one thing… Possibly caring for him in a way that she’d never done with his forebear… They’d tried all afternoon to leave him where he truly belonged, but now Kara felt as though she were stuck between a rock and a hard place. Did she not owe it to the wood to at least try and take care of him? Did she not also owe it to Dog? After everything he had done for her over the course of his life… Denying his pup felt horrible. The more she considered each of the ramifications, the more her fear grew of one painful fact in particular: If she indeed allowed herself to get attached to Pup, and sought to treat him with more latitudes than she or Dog ever allowed themselves, what if something beyond her control came along and took him away again? Could she survive that kind of loss once more? There were other forces to contend with as well… His lack of vaccinating, as well as the threat he posed to humans. Would Alex just convince her to take him to the sanctuary instead? What would that do to him? Kara’s head was absolutely swimming with questions, and it was all just a little bit too much.

    “How about you sleep on it for a night, hm?” Lena asked, pecking a kiss near Kara’s temple.

    The sound of frantic sniffing brought their attention towards the screen door again, and soon they realized that Pup was awake and searching around for a bond again. He began to cry, and then the sound shifted into an attempt at a warbled howl— likely his first. Kara’s eyes started to well with tears, and she felt that she couldn’t stand it anymore. Her bones seemed to want to leap towards the pitiful, despondent howling. She dashed up and away from the couch quickly and scrambled for the door, popping it open to scoop him up into her arms at last. It was as if she couldn’t get there fast enough.

    “No…” Kara decided, a tear rolling off her chin and dropping straight down onto the light fur of Pup’s head, “No, I can’t not be near him now.”

    “Okay. Are you sure you’re up for it?” Lena asked.

    “You don’t understand- I’ve never known the wood to give anything back. It takes because that’s just what it does.” Kara shook her head, “We don’t have a choice, Lena. We have to take care of him. I mean, I want to.”

    “Alright.” Lena nodded with a smile, “Then we will.”

     

     

    “Wow, he really did, didn’t he?” Alex mused out loud from where she leaned up against Kara’s kitchen counter. She was watching Lena and Jaimie attempt to play tug of war with Pup, but all he seemed to want to do was climb again and again into Lena’s lap. The living room was full of giggles from where Alex, Kara, and Maggie could hear in the kitchen.

    “Yup.” Kara agreed, arms folded beside her sister.

    Maggie chimed in, “And he just…”

    Kara nodded. “Mhm. Right away. It was the most incredible thing to witness.”

    “How much more of him do you think is out there?” Alex asked with a shrug, “Dog, I mean.”

    “Mm, not sure.” Kara lied through the slight purse of her lips.

    “Don’t you wanna look?”

    “Babe… you promised.” Maggie said from the stove where she stirred.

    Alex raised her hands in defense, “I’m not carting anyone away to wolf jail here! I’m just curious.”

    Some sharp, excited yips came from the living room right about then. Pup had figured out how to give chase; Jaimie was squealing just a step in front of him, pretending to fall down and playfight over and over again.

    “He’s gonna get huge, Kara. And fast, too.” Alex said.

    Kara rubbed idly at the back of her neck, knowing that her sister was right more often than not about these things. “I know.”

    “There’s gonna be some wild still left in him that you’re gonna have to eventually deal with. Instinctual things, like hunting.”

    Kara bared her teeth with the thought. God forbid any of the neighborhood cats wander near her house in the coming months. Or, ever again, for that matter.

    “Listen, you can still change your mind…”

    Kara shook her head at that. She knew her sister was only trying to do what was best for Pup, but Kara felt that she could be that for him as well. It was a feeling that ran thick in her blood. Explaining it felt impossible, but Alex would just have to understand in her own way. Kara knew that she would eventually. She would just have to see it firsthand for herself, like always.

    “No. We’re up for it.” Kara confessed.

    “Okay, then. You’re gonna have to be more careful than you’ve ever been in your entire life…”

    “Yeah, I’m aware.”

    Alex laughed when Pup suddenly sneezed so hard that he knocked himself down. “He’s super cute, though—”

    Maggie seemed to agree with that and turned her attention away from the stove again, “Isn’t he?!”

    All of a sudden there was a brisk -knock- at the door. Loud and rapid, like someone were repeatedly banging the side of their fist against it. Kara leapt towards the entryway immediately, wanting to make the racket stop as fast as possible. She would be surprised if her front door could take any more abuse…

    -knock-

    -knock knock knock-

    -knock knock knock bang ban—

    “Holy crap- hang on!” Kara called out as she swung the door open.

    Winn dove inside with his hands above his head, pawing frantically across his neck and his chest as if something were crawling on him. Pup noticed him straight away and found the sound of his voice again in the form of a bark, and then a growl. His small but surprisingly territorial frame hopped clumsily in front of Lena with a simultaneous raise of the soft baby hackles along his spine. Kara would have found it endearing if her best friend since childhood wasn’t currently dancing around her entryway like a maniac covered in bees.

    “Winn! Jesus- What are you—”

    “It’s happening!” He wiped downward from the sides of his face, “It’s actually happening, Kara, you gotta help me—”

    “What’s happening? Winn—” Her arms went out to catch him, “…calm down!”

    “The moss!” He screeched once he dug around inside one of his front pockets. His fist then came up to unfurl and exposed a clump of dried, yellowish moss. It seemed to be practically falling right off of him. From the top of his head, to his shoulders, to the bits that were stuck beneath the collar of his shirt… He must have walked underneath a low hanging branch without realizing it. “The moss,” He went on, “It’s everywhere!”

    “What are you talking about?” Kara asked as she gripped him by the shoulders. All throughout their frantic interaction, Alex seemed to be struggling to keep herself from laughing. She covered her mouth with her hand and snickered quietly from behind it.

    “I’m turning into Moss Man!” Winn drawled helplessly.

    “Moss Man?” Kara huffed and narrowed her eyes, “Are you kidding me right now?”

    “I’m serious! Look, you know that grumpy cop that likes to lurk around the parking meters downtown? I sorta told him off- I didn’t mean to be parked so long! I grabbed some moss from the willow- you know the one in front of the post office…? I stuffed it into the tail pipe of his patrol car and now—” His hands went to ruffle through his hair once again, “Now I’m paying for it!”

    Alex had waltzed up to the both of them during Winn’s rather terrified explanation and bent down to pick something up off the floor near his shoes. She then leaned to squint closely at the side of Winn’s head, grin ever apparent upon her face. “I’ll say,” She revealed the tuft of moss in her hand, “…It’s coming out of your ears.”

    Winn turned almost completely white, and then the lids of his eyes fell with a rapid flutter. Kara caught him just in time. She turned to her sister with an admonishing hiss, “Alex!”

    “I’m sorry!” Alex laughed from her belly, “I couldn’t help it!”

    Jaimie laughed from the living room; Granted, it would have been funny to Kara too if it had been anyone else. Winn came to with a gasp before long and then stood up straight again. His legs hadn’t seemed to go completely out from underneath him. He began to claw at his shirt all of a sudden, and there it went, tossed over the top of a lamp shade. He was pale in the warm, yellow light of Kara’s humble abode, red faced and hopping around to take his belt from his pants next.

    “I gotta use your shower—”

    “Alright, go!” Kara gave him a push, “But don’t use my towel!”

    “Or mine, please!” Lena managed to clap a hand over Jaimie’s eyes once Winn made it past them, one cheek of his tighty-whities out from his britches already. Alex was still hee-hawing and doubled over next to Kara, and Kara punched her firmly there upon the round of her shoulder in retaliation.

    “Ow!”

    “You’re a terror, sometimes.”

    “Hey, you remember that entire summer he followed me around with that cricket chirp device he made in shop class?”

    “Oh yeah,” Kara huffed a laugh, “That was fu—” Then she cleared her throat, “I see your point.”

    “He’ll survive.”

    “Excuse me, but,” Lena stood up from where she’d been sat upon the living room floor with Jaimie and Pup, “What’s a moss man?”

    Kara reared her head back in realization. She’d not told this story yet… In fact, there were many tales that Lena hadn’t been made aware of yet from their west end neck of the woods. That was a thing that would probably take a lifetime.

    “It’s, um…” Kara turned to look back at Maggie for permission, considering the fact that Jaimie was still probably just as innocent to these kinds of stories as Lena was. Maggie gave a momentary tilt of her head and then nodded with her “go ahead”. Kara sighed, “It’s just another silly story. A guy gets followed around by Spanish moss after doing something that he shouldn’t, then he starts to notice it everywhere- in his pockets, in the crust of his eyes whenever he wakes up, and then when he dies, his family finds his body stuffed full of it instead of like, bones and stuff.”

    Maggie craned her head from the kitchen to speak towards her daughter in the living room, “Not true, Jaimie. Okay?”

    “Okay…” The wide-eyed girl said back.

    It was likely that Maggie knew there would only be so much she could do in order to protect little Jaimie here, living all the way out in the boondocks where things just simply had a way of “being”. Kara assumed that Alex had already given her the rundown on the applicable dangers: Alligators, the dark, old buildings, and the stories that came with each.

    “Well,” Lena dusted her hands as she stood up and joined Kara to peck a kiss into the hollow of her cheek, “I think maybe we could leave these kinds of stories out whenever it comes to our kids.”

    “Oh, of course, they’re—” Kara rambled to a stop. She gulped loudly and then fumbled her way back towards the shape of words, “Our- our kids?”

    “Sure. Imagine gorgeous blonde hair and little farmers tans running around everywhere.”

    “Yeah,” Alex agreed with an almost too-happy smirk, “…and all of the band-aids!”

    Chapter End Notes

    Thanks for reading. <3 One more chapter to go.

    Here’s to You, Belle Layotte

    Chapter Summary

    Kara greets the supernatural, once and for all.

    Chapter Notes

    Chapter Seventeen: Here’s to You, Belle Layotte

     

    At least once a week she laid here beneath the dripping moss of their great big pine tree. The one where she’d laid her oldest friend to rest. Her idle get-away. Her newfound source of respite.

    Kara shifted her head upon the crook of her arm and then ran her fingers through the small tufts of wild grass that grew up between his many stones. The ones she’d stacked to honor his memory for years and years to come. Her dearest companion. Her truest reflection of herself.

    She’d talked and talked until she found that she had nothing left to say. She thought at first that she had simply because he would rather her silence instead of her conversation while he was alive, and now that he had no choice but to listen to the continued vibrations of her voice, she thought that maybe, just maybe, that it was okay…

    Keeping track of the time was a shade more difficult than she’d realized, though, what with all the actual shade the treetops provided over their quiet resting place. Kara had come to lay here and visit with Dog many times already— So much so that she’d lost count in the many weeks that he’d been gone. More like months, now. It was warm for such an early morning, that much she knew for certain. Once it began to feel like an oven, she swore that she would leave. But not yet… Her eyes were heavy. Maybe she would nap for a spell? Maybe she would wake up again, and all of it would have been just a very bad dream. Kara felt her hand slide against some of the smooth, stacked stone as she began to drift closer and closer towards sleep, and then she gave a wide yawn with her decision just to give in to it. It would all be fine…

    If not for the yawn that traveled back to her ears once she’d closed her own mouth.

    Kara’s eyes darted open again, blinking once more against her hazy half-slumber. Her head felt heavier than usual. Foggy, even. She shook herself and tried to blink a bit more past all of her bleariness, but the glare from the angled rays of sunshine through the moistened corners of her eyes made seeing anything clearly quite impossible.

    She knew the urgency of the situation; The tale that was close at hand…

    Get up… She argued inwardly, forcing her arms to push herself up and away from the ground. Get up, dammit!

    Both of her knees finally upon solid earth, Kara managed to hoist herself up to one foot, and then another. She stood, wobbling a bit from side to side as if the earth craned right and then left beneath her. She didn’t care which way she’d left her swamper… Any direction was a good direction if it got her away from whatever awfulness that had come over her so suddenly. Each of her boots plod clumsily in front of herself as if her legs were asleep, and the more she reached out to swipe at something to grab onto, the more it revealed itself to be further away than she thought. The trees were avoiding her, pulling further and further apart like a tearing seam that opened into pure whiteness—

    And then she fell.

    Water went over her head.

    …Or perhaps, her body fell through it, but from the right way up.

    Bewildered, Kara clawed with her open hands until she broke the surface and gasped for air. Much to her surprise, she was treading deep water in all of her clothes, including her boots. Utter confusion didn’t even come close to describing the whirlwind between her ears. Kara tilted her head up and choked and sputtered for air. Had she somehow blundered all the way back to shore in what felt like so few steps? And since when was the water this deep around this part of the lake? Finally, the malleable surface of mud ran up beneath the toe of one of her boots, and Kara followed the shallow incline there towards higher ground.

    She climbed up and out of the water, dripping and coughing for mercy like a drowned rat. Her hair pulled from her eyes, and her glasses righted upon her nose once more, Kara was able to finally blink again and to take in her surroundings. She was atop a fern dotted shoal, an island presumably to have popped up out of nowhere— but what stopped her once she was free of the treacherous murk was the complete lack of sound she’d made upon her way out.

    There should have been splashing.

    Or, hell, even the sound of all of her coughing and choking…

    …But there was simply nothing.

    Kara realized then how heavily she was breathing. Shouldn’t she be able to hear it? The absence of noise from each breath as they raked in and out of her lungs made her heart pound even harder… Feeling it so fiercely from behind her ears was one thing, but not being able to hear it?

    Fucking terrifying.

    Kara knelt down and smacked her hand flat against the top of the water, sending it splashing upwards at her without so much as a single sound. Panic set into overdrive, the slosh inside of her boots pulled heavy at her feet once she went to turn and run, but something large obstructed her escape. She tripped and fell straight into the flat bottom of a jon boat. The lack of noise from doing so was just as unsettling as everything else.

    This was a nightmare… It had to be. Kara pushed herself back up and then saw that something was in the boat with her. A great mass of vines had grown up there through the rotted square planks of wood and coiled tightly around something—

    No, not something. Someone.

    Come and gone like the sweep of Amarillo rain, her breath had all but left her. Whoever it was in the boat appeared to be small, perhaps even childlike in length. They were covered from head to toe with vines all except for their face, and Kara leaned upward to take a better look. To her horror, she found that it was a child. A young girl with vibrant, black skin. She was asleep longways and wrapped up into the vines, swaddled like a papoose. Kara reached out to touch the fleshy part of her cheek, nearly leaping at the fact that she was, indeed, warm.

    Kara’s lips finally parted to mouth the word “hey”, but like everything else, the sound from it simply chose not to exist. She thought to herself how crazy this was… There was no way all of this could be real. She must have rolled over onto some mushroom spores or something while she had laid next to Dog’s grave. That, or she was having the most vivid and intense dream she’d ever had in her entire life… Kara kept trying to talk to the girl as she pulled frantically at the vines that were wrapped around her, but still her words went out into thin air. Her father’s pocketknife came loose from its clip at her front pocket, and then she began to cut, more and more until she could pull the girl loose and cradle her safely into her arms. Kara was horrified… The girl was alive, and from what she could tell straight away, seemingly in perfect health as well. Her state of dress was almost a patchwork slip, floppy lacework doilies in the shape of flowers sewn in and around its hem. Hair a natural bouffant with a pale blue ribbon, she slept soundly in the space of Kara’s arms and stirred not even once for how hard Kara had worked to pull her free. Kara stood up with her and looked around, horrified now for a complete other reason…

    She had no memory of this place.

    Kara knew every westerly inch of the Caddo by heart. Every cypress, every crane, and every boulder... but there was more water here than she’d seen on any part of the lake before, deep middle or east Louisiana end. What she saw next made her knees feel like they would all but collapse out from underneath herself.

    There in the distance the Washington chugged past, stately and mobile and painted with its pristine blue lettering upon its bow: 1861.

    Kara felt her lips move again as she blustered silently for some sort of an explanation. She came up with nothing. It was all she could do just to watch helplessly as the paddle steamer churned the wake of the water behind it, coal gray smoke out from its stacks and everything.

    You’re asleep, Kara… She tried reasoning with herself. You have to wake up!

    The child in her arms felt so real, though… She couldn’t just stand there and allow any more of the absurdity in her midst to continue. Something about this place felt entirely separate, as if scooped right out of time and bottled up like a fresh caught tadpole. Nothing about it made any other kind of sense.

    Kara’s feet throttled forward to plunge into the water again, knowing the only way out would have to be “through” much like the way she had gotten there to begin with, but her boots, for whatever reason, refused to go under. The lake bottom had come up to meet her somehow, and with one careful step after another, she was into the trees again, and the sounds of summer cicadas soon graced her ears once more.

    Kara looked down, and the girl yawned sleepily in her arms. At least she’d heard it.

    Thank you… She cried inwardly. Thank you, God…

    Bearings regained and motivation to run found once again, the girl stayed sound asleep the rest of the way through the woods and into Kara’s swamper, past each grunted pull of the oars and each eventual step up the tall stairs of Kara’s back porch…

    Lena met them at the door and quickly came to a skidded halt as she went to step through it. She blinked at Kara’s disheveled state and seemed to be unable to tear her eyes away from the sleeping child there in her muddy, muscled arms. Kara had never been so fond of the sound of her own voice now that she was able to hear herself speak again…

    “I can explain.” She looked down and then gave an unsure shrug of her shoulders, “Actually, no. I can’t.”

     

     

    “Take this here, cher. Don’t leave it out to sit on the counter all day, either.”

    Lena smiled over the aluminum take-away casserole dish in her hands, still half full of its wonderful smelling contents. “Oh, Mrs. Henshaw this smells heavenly—”

    “Megan, please. We have met before.”

    Hank’s wife was, admittedly, a woman rich with maternal instincts. Her no-nonsense attitude paired with many a heartfelt affirmation had made her Uncertain’s most beloved schoolteacher for years now. Kara gave a small yet gleeful pump of her fist at the offering of food. The woman could cook, too.

    “Where is she?” Megan asked once she draped the handle of her purse over the tall back of one of Kara’s dining chairs.

    Kara took the casserole dish from Lena and nudged into the living room with the point of her chin. “She took up some of Jaimie’s dolls once we got her to calm down. All I could understand were the words ‘papa’ and ‘dinner’… I think.”

    Megan went around the couch and into the living room where the little girl played, awake now and startled beyond reasoning with. It was the unmistakable Creole-like dialect which she spoke in that had prompted Kara’s decision to call Hank, who then suggested his wife to be better suited to parley in between. It seemed the young girl didn’t know a single drop of English. Hank stood beside the kitchen table with Kara and Lena while Megan attempted to engage in conversation nearby. His face was pulled with a tightness in his brow, and his arms were folded over the broad expanse of his chest as if he were very unsettled.

    “How did you say you found her, again? Is she titit- very small? Or…” He trailed off to lean inconspicuously towards the living room, giving the term “rubbernecking” a whole new meaning. Hank was right to be cautious. The girl seemed to be afraid of many things, all except for Kara. She’d shied away from the tap as it ran water into her glass and had even turned her nose up at bottled ketchup once Lena had tried to feed her.

    “She’s gotta be something like seven or eight at least.” Kara shrugged. “And she was just… there. In the woods.” She said as her hands came down to -smack- lightly against her thighs.

    Telling the truth would only make her sound crazy. Hell, what she’d gone through to discover the child was crazy enough as it is, and almost too burdensome even to ponder for a single moment. Kara’s stomach turned at the mere memory of it. It was still too fresh upon her mind. Lena had asked and asked, but Kara felt that there were some parts of the experience that would simply die with her. She had, of course, omitted the part about the very lively and in-use Washington, as well as the temporary deafness, and the vines that had covered the girl as if they’d grown over her like that for many, many years… She found her asleep in the woods in a derelict boat, and that was all anyone needed to know. Kara still had a hard time believing the rest of it herself. The girl was living proof that at least some of it had happened. Above all else, that was what bothered her to high heavens.

    “Son dòmi?” Hank asked. When Kara puzzled her own brow in confusion, he clarified, “Sound asleep?”

    “Yeah—” Kara huffed, “Like a lanky little rock.”

    The sound of small crying came from the living room then, and Hank caught Kara by the elbow upon her way there. The instinctual way her feet wanted to move towards the girl’s rescue again had surprised them both. Hank shook his head, “She is fine.”

    Kara knew that he was right. She looked down at her phone that was face up on the kitchen table and sighed when there still didn’t seem to be a reply from Alex. Her sister had been her first call, but it was likely that she was out in the field somewhere and far from cell service. Boy, would she be in for a surprise… Kara had left a message with Maggie, so it was only a matter of time before the sound of Alex’s bootsteps would make it to her front door in a godawful hurry.

    After a while, Megan returned to the kitchen with a completely different demeanor present upon her face. The stark difference of it nearly made Kara’s head spin. So many things had assaulted her view of the “impossible” that day, and it rattled her to her bones to see Megan —one of the most unshakable women she’d ever known— pulled so loose from her own threads of sanity. Her mouth dropped open and closed at first in search of where to begin, and then she shook her head, presumably just to go with what had shocked her first.

    “She can’t recall her name, or anyone else’s. I asked about her family, and she began to cry for her papa, but she doesn’t remember where she lives. She did, however, have many more questions for me that were uniquely concerning.”

    “As in?” Lena asked quietly.

    “As in, if my husband and I are ‘free’, for starters.”

    “Free…” Kara mumbled almost incoherently.

    “The buttons on her dress,” Megan shifted uncomfortably and clasped her hands in front of herself, “…they’re made of wood.” She paused for another moment, and then she continued, “And she speaks in a dialect of Creole that doesn’t really exist anymore.”

    As always, Lena’s rationally thinking brain sought to bring forth reason. “Her family must have raised her to—”

    “You don’t understand, she mentioned the word ‘choupique’ when I asked her what she was doing before she couldn’t remember anything else. It’s a Native American word meaning ‘mud fish’, a term that’s rarely used today. She doesn’t speak Haitian Creole like most black folks do here on the Caddo. She speaks ‘true’ Louisiana Creole… The old kind.”

    “Well, I’m…” Kara flustered at last, “I’m absolutely lost.”

    Megan only resumed her pensive, mile-away stare as if she were considering something completely outlandish. Her husband seemed to recognize the look right away. Hank turned to her and they spoke quietly with one another— long enough to make Kara’s stomach churn again with every other thought of what might come next. They had to find the child’s family. She’d spoken of her father, but where on earth was he? Lena was usually the one to talk her through one awful thing at a time in most sticky situations, taking the burden of making a plan straight from her shoulders, but Kara still felt that there was only so much her mind would allow her to share before it would all but break. It’s not that she didn’t want to. It was the “how to” that plagued her.

    “Kara.” Megan finally returned to (hopefully) offer more insight. “I’m troubled by this.”

    “You and me both.” Kara huffed.

    “Did you know that the Caddo tribes often offered refuge to runaway slaves in the early nineteenth century?”

    “I- I didn’t. No.”

    “You would benefit from my history class, cher.”

    “I don’t doubt it.”

    “Other tribes such as the Cherokee and the Choctaw saw this as an affront to their wishes of being deemed “civilized” by the American colonists. To them, it was a step in the wrong direction and further created tension between Native nations and the Americans, because the sentiment back then would always be ‘red skin is still red skin’.”

    Kara shifted uncomfortably and went to sit down with a -creak- from one of her dining chairs. “Why are you telling me this?”

    “True Louisiana Creole is one of the only evolved pidgin languages that incorporates Native American words as well as French words. The reason it’s so endangered is because there’s barely anyone left to speak it.”

    “I feel like I’m still missing the point—”

    “There were raids in the run up to the civil war, the Five Civilized Tribes- Cherokee, Chickasaw, Choctaw, Creek and Seminole nations- they massacred most of the Caddo tribes for their aiding the Union’s cause and for hiding black slaves.”

    “Wait…” Kara laughed with a feeling of delirium, throwing both of her hands up in pause, “You can’t be saying—”

    “I don’t think anyone is looking for her, because I think they are all gone.”

    Kara’s heart plummeted. She then stood up quickly, enough so to scrape the legs of her chair noisily against the hard laminate floor of her kitchen. She began to pace. Lena tried reaching out for her upon each pass, offering more gentle words that no longer fell in the shape of “reason”.

    “Consider it all, Kara. She’s freaked out by the tv, the tap, she doesn’t speak a word of English, even her clothes are—”

    “Have you all—” Kara clasped her hands hard in front of her chest and then shook them until her knuckles turned white, “…lost your damn minds?”

    “Kara—”

    “Have I hit my head or something?” Kara perked up at the thought and turned quickly to Lena, her featherlight panic slowly setting in once again, “I hit my head, didn’t I?”

    “No, Kara.”

    “Then, no. Absolutely not. I won’t even consider it. I’ve lived here my whole life and I know how strange things can get, and how a lot of things can get twisted into stories and how some things just don’t have any sort of explanation—” She jabbed a finger back towards the living room and then spoke with a quiet, frustrated hiss, “But you cannot tell me that I plucked this little girl right out of time- almost two hundred years ago. Oh, I get it- I suppose you’re gonna tell me that she’s—”

    “Little Yawning Girl?” Hank said with yet another stern look upon his face.

    Kara huffed loudly as she sat back down again, “You too, Hank?”

    He sent his palms up as if there were nothing else he could do, “I think we are all just as confused as you.”

    “Oh, believe me,” Kara shook her head, feeling silly enough to want to suddenly blurt the parts that she had left out, “Confused doesn’t even begin to describe it.”

    “Well. For what it’s worth,” Megan added, “I’ve never known a child to lie through tears like that. Just imagine what she must be going through.”

    With that, Kara all but clammed up. Her head went into her hands, and her knee began to bounce. It had been one thing after another since that morning and she was becoming more and more exhausted as time went on. True to style, the rapid -thunk- of bootheels across the deck of her front porch skidded to a halt at the door all of a sudden, and there Alex gave her panicky turn of the knob to burst right in. Kara saw the ribboned top of the young girl’s head dart backwards in surprise, and Alex began to pant tiredly as if she were out of breath, almost like she’d not stopped running until she’d made it there.

    “I’m here—” She huffed, “I’m here.” Alex caught herself with her mouth hanging slightly open as she laid her eyes upon the girl in front of her. Then she looked over at Kara with an astonished expression. “Tell me everything.”

     

     

    Kara did not, in fact, tell her sister “everything”.

    There were some things about living on the Caddo that she would never tell another soul, not for as long as she lived. At the top of that list: the silent jar of horrors from where she rescued one nearly two-hundred-year-old sleeping girl.

    “Tell them to stop,” Lena had begged the attending nurse at the pediatrics wing of Bossier City Medical, “Tell them to stop crowding her in there or let me go inside!”

    Kara’s recollection of the days that came after haunted her every waking moment.

    “A group home? Wait- why are the police here? Stop- don’t- leave her alone—!"

    It had taken every ounce of strength not to shove the social worker’s pen where the sun didn’t shine after Kara was finally allowed to sign upon a dotted line. Getting her home was one thing, but getting her comfortable was… well, another.

    She cried.

    Profusely, and without end.

    For entire days —from sunup to sundown— Kara would set out in her swamper and try to make her way back to the sandy shoal where the girl’s fallen apart jon boat had kept her. Each long pull of her oars set upon a new question in her mind. Who was she really? Where was the father she kept crying out for? How, or even why had Kara found her in the first place? If everything that Megan had told her was true, and history had played out exactly the way that she’d described, then why had the girl been spared the fate of her family? Not to mention the people who gave them refuge? Had the wood kept her safe and sound for a reason? Had it known what was to befall the Caddo all along? If so, then it must have waited all that time for someone worthy enough to come along. Perhaps Dog and Pup were tests… It seemed like no matter how much Kara fantasized upon the impossible, that was where it always seemed to land: The impossible.

    Kara was more unsettled about the supernatural state of her home than she’d ever been. It was like seeing the Caddo in a whole new light. There was heeding a timeless tale of caution, and then there was believing it. She had always reserved just a sliver of doubt for most things, because ghosts and werewolves and lake monsters and shapeless sentient forest guardians couldn’t possibly exist…

    Could they?

    Each day that passed, Kara would row back to her lily pad scattered shoreline with more questions than she had answers for. Nobody was looking for this little girl. Nobody even knew she had existed. Nobody living, that is.

    Kara trudged disappointedly up the stairs of her back porch and then slunk heavily down into a cushioned wicker chair, letting her tired arms hang over the sides for how hard they had worked that day. She’d gone out with the rising of the sun and only made it back before its setting. The subtle -click- of the screen door’s latch and -pop- of its spring finally gave way enough for Lena to poke her head out.

    She came out with her hands upon her hips, buffeting a length of her hair from her face with an equally exhausted breath. “Anything?” Lena asked.

    Kara shook her head. “There’s nothing. No tracks, no shelters, no signs of life at all. How is she?”

    “Asleep, finally.” Lena answered as she sat in the chair beside Kara, “We did learn that ketchup isn’t actually so bad, though.”

    “Progress.” Kara smiled, and then she frowned, “I’m sorry I left you alone all day again.”

    Lena reached over to rub at Kara’s sore shoulder, “I wasn’t alone. Pup’s always just a few steps away.” She laughed. “She’s still a bit scared of him, actually…”

    Kara sighed. “What are we gonna do?”

    “Whatever you feel that’s best. That’s what.”

    “I don’t want her to go to some home…”

    “I assumed as much each time we get a visit from child protective services. If they keep showing up unannounced the way they have, then I’m not sure that we can keep Pup a secret for much longer—”

    “We could do it.”

    Lena stopped. “You mean…?”

    “If you wanted to, of course.”

    “Kara, are you saying—”

    “I can’t think of a better place for her other than here. If she can’t have the family she wants, then… we could at least be the family she needs. Right?”

    Lena’s cheeks turned a shade of red, and her eyes began to grow misty. She smiled from ear to ear at Kara then as if she were pleased just to hear those words in that exact order. “Right.”

    “We can give her everything.”

    “And so much more, I’m sure.”

     

     

    —Ten Years Later—

     

    The tell-tale spinning of tires from a lead-footed bus driver let Kara know that she was home. They were all packed and ready for the weekend to come; A quick summer vacation to celebrate an upcoming graduation.

    “Layotte!” Kara called down from the kitchen window, to which she received no answer. The bus had stopped, so Kara knew that she was there… but the lack of a reply always, always set her just a little bit on edge. For ten years now she’d dreaded the sudden threat of another vanishing act. The taking away of a gift given. The profound sense of loss that would be felt for the one she called “daughter”.

    There was a -stomp- near the front door, and then a screech that came next. Kara bolted from her dishes at the kitchen sink. “Layotte?” She called out again, nearly missing the end of the couch as she juked around it for the door, “La—”

    The door came open, and there sat a mass of slightly chewed feathers upon the mat in front of it.

    Chicken and all.

    …Left as a gift.

    Layotte pulled her headphones down over her braids with a disgusted face towards the poor creature, and then she gave a quick twist of her hips to shout into the distance, “Dangit, Pup—!"

    Kara felt like laughing and crying all at the same time. Seeing her there, just as she was, hearing her voice, feeling the sense of déjà vu that she felt… It was enough to overwhelm her. Kara lurched through the threshold of their front door and wrapped her arms around her daughter— backpack rumpled and shoulders squeezed as tightly as she could manage.

    “Mom…” Layotte began to tap, “Mom… Mom!”

    “Oh.” Kara let go.

    “Can’t—” Layotte continued teasing as if she were being choked to death, “…breathe…”

    “Sorry, boo.”

    “Why do you do that?” Layotte shrugged one strap of her backpack over her shoulder again and then stepped inside just to discard it with a toss against the end of the couch.

    “…Do what?”

    “I dunno, grab me up like you haven’t seen me in years?”

    “What, I can’t just be excited that you’re home?”

    “You saw me this morning.” Layotte drawled around a bite of fresh-baked biscuit. She’d tucked three of them into her hands and chewed happily around a satisfied hum.

    Kara’s eyebrows went up with a warning, “Your mom’s gonna have your hide for that…”

    “She’ll get over it. Aren’t we taking them with us on the road anyway?”

    “Yeah, that’s if you leave any of them left—” Kara gave a swat to Layotte’s hand once she reached for another, “I swear, you might not have been born of me, but you sure do have my appetite. You know how long the trip is to New Orleans.”

    Layotte shrugged with a laugh. “Where is she, anyway?” She asked as she looked out another window towards the garden down below, “I need y’all to sign some stuff for college—”

    “Oh, darlin’ she’s gonna be all over that as soon as you bring it up. Let it just breathe for now, hm? Enjoy the time off you have before you start school all over again.”

    “You just don’t wanna talk about dormitory housing.”

    “Nope.” Kara admitted with a pop to the letter “p”. She shook her head, “Not one bit.”

    Layotte sighed and then put herself right in front of Kara’s path, forcing her mother to look up from her boots. She reached down and squeezed Kara’s hands into her own; a lovely contrast of smooth sable and sun-kissed fawn. “When are you gonna stop being so afraid of me leaving?”

    “…Never.”

    “I don’t know what to do with that, mom.”

    “Too bad, then.” Kara smiled, “I love you forever.”

    Layotte rolled her eyes with a final sigh of defeat. “I love you forever too.”

    Chapter End Notes

    There you have it. Layotte's name, for those that remember, was sung to Kara and Lena in Blackberry Cabin earlier on in the story. Let's say she has Kara's appetite, and Lena's aptitude for science, and is very, very happy. I like to wrap most of my stories up in a neat little bow, something to tie it all back to the beginning whether its a phrase, or sentiment, or joke... Full circle is what I like.

    Thanks to everyone who's tuned in from start to finish, and if you're making it here now in the coming months after the story's been completed, thanks to you, too. I've appreciated all of the wonderful comments throughout. This one was so much fun for me, and very soothing to boot. I would not at all mind if you recommended this story to friends/people who you think may enjoy it. I’m always glad to know if the thing gets read.

    I'm still interested in a Sanvers story to run alongside this one, but that won't be for a while. No idea how much time I'm needing in between fics these days. The world is coo coo crazy and I'm just tryin' to live in it.

    Be good. See you next time.
    ABNL

    Bonus: Map Image

    Caddo Lake Whoopsie Daisies takes place on the wooded west end of this map that I have created, marked "Caddo West". Many thousand acres of bald cypress trees are partly submerged here in the murky still of the lake, denoted by the word "Cypress". Further south is the city of Uncertain, Texas (pinned with a red star). South by southwest from there are the woods that back up to Kara's lake cottage, which fill out into many more thousands of acres of pineywoods forest. This map is (or barely passes for) topographically accurate. There is a preserve/wildlife refuge west of the Uncertain star that is also not marked upon this map.

    The "Middle Deep" is true to its name, as the deepest parts of the lake are most often found within its middle. The log jam (otherwise known as the "Great Raft") via the Red River created Caddo Lake by spilling into the lowlands of the Cypress Valley between the states of Texas and Louisiana, somewhere around the years 1811-1812. Its waters have receded over a period of two hundred years, but infrequent flooding due to tropical storms from the Gulf of Mexico bearing southeast, as well as heavy rainy seasons in general, make it difficult to establish just how much.

    There are, to date, over 218 reported "Bigfoot" sightings in the state of Texas alone, most of which were purported to occur either on or near the Caddo. I can assure you, at least double (or perhaps even triple) that amount are not reported to any reputable park or authority service. The Middle Deep possesses one large landmass surrounded by dark waters, known as "Tar Island". In the foggy months, fisherman have reported the island to randomly disappear. The "Louisiana East" lies across the Texas/Louisiana border, and is abundant with calm, timber-laden waters ideal for shallow jon boat fishing. 

    In this AU, Alex works and lives on the northwest end of the lake, marked "Caddo Lake Wildlife Management Area".

    Tar Island would be a reasonable guess as to the location of Layotte's boat, for it is rumored to not only disappear, but to move about the Caddo at will.

    The Washington Paddle Steamer (not actually scuttled on the Caddo, sorry to say) I imagine to be somewhere around the pale blue northwest bend of the lake, under the Caddo Lake Wildlife Management Area.

    If you have any questions as to where a certain feature of this story might lie upon this map (however, structures like Blackberry Cabin for example, one can safely assume would reside in or around the star of Uncertain) then feel free to ask in the comments. I will update the map accordingly for every pin added, if applicable. Otherwise, I will do my best to reply to your query in the comment section of this chapter.

    I do plan to come back to this AU in the future, I am just not sure in what capacity (pairing tag, POV, etc.) just yet. If you wish to stay up to date, you can subscribe via my author page. In doing so, you will receive email notifications for any and all works/chapters that I post here on AO3. Thanks again for reading.
